B.2    Proof of the step-drop lemma
Proof. If η = 0, take W = n and E = R = 1. A zero-variable symbol is also trivial, so assume that
the box has at least one variable and that η ̸= 0. We induct on s. If s = 1, the group is a vector
group and verticality gives F (x) = c e(η(x)) with |c| ≤ ep . Choose a rational vector v of controlled
                                        P
height with η(v) = 1. Write log X(t) = i ai ti . The product formula for the mean of a linear phase
and the geometric-sum bound give integers qi such that

                                         |η(ai ) − qi | ≤ e2p /(2Ti ).

Indeed the product of the n normalized geometric sums has magnitude at least e−2p , so each
individual magnitude is at least e−2p . Take slow coefficients (η(ai ) − qi )v, rational coefficients qi v,
and middle coefficients in ker η. This proves the base case.

The differentiated symbol. Assume s ≥ 2. Apply the weighted-shift selection in the proof of
Lemma A.9, after rescaling F to be unit-bounded. Together with Lemma A.8, it gives at least
B(p)−1 i Ti distinct shifts h, with |hi | ≤ Ti , for which the normalized square test has a mean of
        Q

magnitude at least B(p)−1 on its overlap, and whose individual side lengths are at least B(p)−1 Ti .
    Write nj = gj /gj+1 . The associated graded Lie algebra of the quotient square has diagonal
layers nj , 1 ≤ j < s, and relative layers nj+1 in square degree j. The relative part is abelian in this
associated graded algebra: the bracket of two relative inputs has one extra filtration degree. The
diagonal acts on it by the ordinary adjoint action.
    Use the normalization in Lemma A.8, writing

                                         (log X)1 (h) = eh + mh ,

where eh has controlled size and mh lies in a fixed rational grid. For a symbol C write Yh (C) =
(∂h C)C −1 for its right logarithmic derivative. In relative-then-diagonal semidirect coordinates, the
square symbol is
                                  (X≤s−1 , Yh (X) − eh − AdX mh ) .                              (B.7)

                                                      96
Here the relative component in original layer j is its homogeneous parameter part of degree j − 1,
and the horizontal part is zero. Here is a formal verification, including the case where the original
filtration has no grading. Choose complements Ej of gj+1 in gj and let δz act by z j on Ej . The
brackets
                                      [u, v]z = δz [δz−1 u, δz−1 v]
are polynomial in z: a component in Ek of a bracket of Ei , Ej is multiplied by z k−i−j , with k ≥ i + j.
At z = 0 this is the associated graded bracket. Write expz , logz for the finite Baker–Campbell–
Hausdorff group with this bracket. The polynomial

                                         Pz (t) = δz log g(t/z)

has constant term P0 (t) = log X(t). Similarly the scaled normalizing constants are zeh + O(z 2 ) and
zmh + O(z 2 ). Here O(z 2 ) denotes terms divisible by z 2 in a formal polynomial, without any bound
on their coefficients. The scaled relative product is therefore

             expz (−zeh + O(z 2 )) expz (Pz (t + zh)) expz (−zmh + O(z 2 )) expz (−Pz (t)).

When the shift and inserted constants are zero, the middle product equals the identity for every z.
Consequently the first-order variations of the bracket itself and of the subleading coefficients in Pz
cancel identically. The coefficient of z depends only on the displayed shift and insertions, evaluated
in the graded group at z = 0. It can thus be calculated over the dual numbers R[ε]/(ε2 ), where the
exact identity is

             exp(−εeh )X(t + εh) exp(−εmh )X(t)−1 = exp(ε(Yh (X) − eh − AdX mh )) .

Indeed X(t + εh)X(t)−1 = exp(εYh (X)), conjugation of the right insertion gives AdX mh , and
brackets of two ε terms vanish. In original layer j, the coefficient of z is exactly the homogeneous
parameter part of degree j − 1 of the relative logarithm, since δz multiplies that monomial by
z j−(j−1) . This proves (B.7). The same calculation gives the semidirect multiplication rule y + AdX y ′
under a preceding diagonal coordinate X.

A common fast subgroup. Apply Lemma A.4 to the quotient square. One top mode retains
bias B(p)−1 , and its restriction to the relative copy of ns is η. The induction hypothesis factors
its symbol into slow, fast and rational factors. Every resulting fast subalgebra has polynomial
dimension and rational height B(p). There are only B(p) possibilities, since a rational basis is
specified by polynomially many bounded-height entries. Retain a set of B(p)−1 i Ti shifts with the
                                                                                 Q

same fast subalgebra U e.
    Let U be its diagonal projection, with Us = ns , and let Vj ⊆ nj be its kernel in the relative part,
setting V1 = 0. Then
                                    η(Vs ) = 0,    [Ui , Vj ] ⊆ Vi+j .                            (B.8)
All these are controlled rational spaces. For each retained shift, denote the diagonal splitting by
X = Ah Bh Dh through grade s − 1. Fix one retained shift and denote its outside diagonal factors by
A, D. Lemma A.6 puts A−1 Ah and Dh D−1 in U . Lift their logarithms to U       e by a bounded rational
section of the diagonal projection. The first lift is slow, and the second is rational. Absorbing them
into the middle factor makes its diagonal equal to

                                            B = A−1 XD−1

for every retained shift. Extend A, D to original grade s with zero logarithmic component there;
the same formula defines the full symbol B and puts it in U .

                                                   97
   In a subgroup of a semidirect product with abelian kernel, two elements with the same diagonal
have relative coordinates equal modulo the kernel. The fast relative coordinate, denoted ZB , is
therefore common modulo V . Comparing the relative coordinates in (B.7) and using

                           Yh (ABD) = Yh (A) + AdA Yh (B) + AdAB Yh (D)

gives
                                Yh (B) = sh + AdB rh + ZB       (mod V ).                            (B.9)
For clarity, if uh , Zh , vh are the three relative factors, the two sides before solving for Yh (B) are

                          Yh (X) − eh − AdX mh = uh + AdA Zh + AdAB vh .

Thus one can take
                                    sh = AdA−1 (eh + uh − Yh (A)),
                                    rh = vh + AdD mh − Yh (D).

The coefficients of sh in original layer j, parameter degree j − 1, satisfy B(p)T −α bounds. This uses
|hi | ≤ Ti when differentiating the slow symbol A. The coefficients of rh have bounded denominator;
integrality of h is used for Yh (D). These claims include the horizontal constant terms. Pigeonhole
the denominators and subtract one retained instance of (B.9) from every other instance. The term
ZB disappears, the number of distinct shift differences is unchanged up to one, and the side and
coefficient bounds only change by controlled factors.

Extraction of a horizontal kernel. Let M be the direct sum, over j = 1, . . . , s, of the coefficient
spaces of degree-(j − 1) homogeneous polynomials with values in nj /Vj . Choose controlled rational
coordinates. The operator M = AdB on M is block lower unitriangular by Lie layer, and is well
defined by (B.8). The linear map h 7→ Yh (B) takes values in M. Let LT multiply a coefficient of tα
by T α . The horizontal scales are one and every other scale is at least mini Ti .
    The subtracted identities supply the bounded lattice vectors required by Lemma B.2, with a
common grid for the rh ; changing from the chosen rational coordinates to a rectangular grid merely
enlarges its denominator. Consequently there are a controlled rational K ⊆ n1 , linear maps S, R on
K, and, for every coordinate derivative,

                   S = AdB R      (mod V ),       S(k)1 = R(k)1 = k,
                   |LT S(k)| ≤ B(p)|k|,                                                             (B.10)
                   Yi (B) = si + AdB ri + S(ki )    (mod V ),       |LT si | ≤ B(p)/Ti .

Here R on a controlled rational basis and all the ri have bounded common denominator, and
ki ∈ K need not be rational. We henceforth use these identities in the quotient coefficient space, so
equalities of components are always interpreted modulo Vj .

The target subalgebra. We have obtained the derivative system (B.10), with B taking values
in U . Define

                 W1 = {v ∈ K ∩ U1 : [v, K] ⊆ V2 },         Wj = Vj ∩ Uj     (2 ≤ j ≤ s).

These controlled rational spaces form a graded Lie subalgebra. Indeed [W1 , W1 ] ⊆ V2 ∩ U2 , while
every other required bracket lies in the appropriate U layer and in V by (B.8). Also η(Ws ) = 0. It
remains to remove slow and rational factors from B until its logarithmic coefficients lie in W .

                                                    98
   Write Pj = (log B)j , updating this notation after each removal. First we put P1 in U1 ∩ K. At
stage j ≥ 2 we arrange [Pj−1 , K] ⊆ Vj and then put Pj in Uj ∩ Vj . The accompanying derivative
terms must vanish at the same time so that the next layer reduces to an ordinary coefficient equation.
We begin with the two operations that preserve the derivative system.

Removing slow and rational derivatives. The system (B.10) is preserved when B = aB ′ b,
where a is slow and b rational, both in U . The correct transformed quantities are
                            S ′ = Ada−1 S,                   R′ = Adb R,
                                                                                                  (B.11)
                            s′i = Ada−1 (si − Yi (a)),        ri′ = Adb ri − Yi (b).
The identities follow from the product rule for Yi . Their quantitative bounds persist: after coefficient
scaling by T α , multiplication by a slow coefficient only multiplies norms by a controlled factor;
rational products preserve bounded denominators. Also, for ks , kr ∈ K, the replacement

                    si ← si − S(ks ),        ri ← ri − R(kr ),          ki ← ki + ks + kr         (B.12)

preserves the system. Its bounds persist if |ks | ≤ B(p)/Ti and kr has bounded denominator.
   The coefficient of ti in P1 is si,1 + ri,1 + ki ∈ U1 . Modulo the rational space U1 + K, separation
puts si,1 and ri,1 separately in that space. Use a controlled rational decomposition into a U1 part and
a K part. Absorb the K parts by (B.12), and remove the U1 parts by factors a, b whose logarithms
have only layer one. We have arranged

                                   P1 ∈ U1 ∩ K,             si,1 = ri,1 = 0.                      (B.13)

   We now process j = 2, . . . , s. At the start of stage j, suppose the following hold:
                                   Pd ∈ Ud ∩ Vd         (2 ≤ d < j),
                                   [Pd , K] ⊆ Vd+1      (1 ≤ d ≤ j − 2),
                                   Sd = Rd = 0          (2 ≤ d < j),
                                   si,d = ri,d = 0      (1 ≤ d < j).
The last two lines are statements in the quotient by V . Expanding S = AdB R in layer j gives

                          [Pj−1 , k] = Sj (k) − Rj (k)       (mod Vj ),        k ∈ K.             (B.14)

Indeed all lower terms of R except its horizontal part vanish modulo V , and every term in exp(ad P )k
with an inner bracket from a layer at most j − 2 belongs to V .
    Let E = Uj−1 ∩ Vj−1 , or E = U1 ∩ K when j = 2. On a fixed controlled rational basis (kb ) of K,
define the rational linear map
                                            M
                              Qj : E −→          nj /Vj ,       v 7−→ ([v, kb ])b .
                                             b

The right side of (B.14) is a slow coefficient array minus a rational one. Project modulo Qj (E), and
apply Lemma A.5 coefficient by coefficient. Each part lies separately in the image. A controlled
rational section provides homogeneous polynomials aj−1 , bj−1 with values in E such that

                         Qj (aj−1 ) = (Sj (kb ))b ,         Qj (bj−1 ) = −(Rj (kb ))b .

The first is slow and the second rational. Remove their exponentials from the left and right of B.
Formula (B.11) now gives
                                 [Pj−1 , K] ⊆ Vj ,  Sj = Rj = 0.

                                                      99
Lower vanishing conditions persist. When j = 2, the operation creates horizontal parts −∂i a1 and
−∂i b1 in si , ri ; both lie in K. Absorb them by (B.12). Since the new S2 , R2 are already zero modulo
V2 , this restores (B.13) without changing their vanishing. For j > 2, the analogous new parts lie in
Vj−1 and are already zero in the quotient.
     At this point the right logarithmic derivative satisfies

                                       Yi (B)j = ∂i Pj    (mod Vj ).

To verify this, expand it as a finite sum of nested brackets of P and ∂i P . A derivative in a preceding
layer at least two lies in V . A horizontal derivative lies in K, and its innermost bracket lies in
V by the bracket conditions just established. Subsequent brackets preserve V by (B.8). Likewise
(AdB ri )j = ri,j modulo Vj , since all its lower components are already zero there. Thus (B.10)
reduces to
                                       ∂i Pj = si,j + ri,j (mod Vj ).
For every monomial tβ of degree j, choose one i with βi > 0 and divide the corresponding derivative
coefficient by βi . This expresses its coefficient, modulo Vj , as a slow part of size B(p)T −β and a
rational part. Because Pj takes values in Uj , separation modulo Uj + Vj and a bounded rational
section allow both parts to be lifted into Uj . Remove their exponential lifts at layer j. We have
obtained Pj ∈ Uj ∩ Vj . The derivative identity then says si,j + ri,j = 0 modulo Vj ; separation, now
at scale Ti T α , makes the two terms vanish separately. This completes stage j.
    The final logarithmic coefficients now lie in the target algebra W , whose top layer is annihilated
by η. There were only s stages, with joint rational linear algebra at each stage. All corrections
therefore combine into slow and rational outside factors with the stated bounds. Together with the
original A, D, these give the required factorization of X.


C     Shift comparison
Shift comparison lowers the degree of a positive test after multiplying by a translate of an arbitrary
nonnegative function. The degree-one case uses Bohr-set almost-periodicity. At higher degree we
recover the test from two fixed finite lists and apply the induction outside one exceptional set of
shifts. The lower-degree test may then depend on the shift.
    Put G = Z/N Z, with its uniform probability measure, and evaluate nilsequences on the
representatives 0, . . . , N − 1. For d ≥ 0 and q ≥ 2, let Td (q) denote the [0, 1]-valued niltests of degree
at most d and complexity at most q. Thus T0 (q) consists of constants in [0, 1].

Theorem C.1 (Shift comparison). For fixed d ≥ 1 and 0 < ϵ < 1, there is a constant C = C(d, ϵ)
such that the following holds. Suppose that p ≥ 2, N ≥ exp((2 + p)C ) is odd, and 0 ≤ f, g, J ≤ ep .
Set P = (2 + p)C . If
                            En (f − g)(n)T (n) ≤ e−P    (T ∈ Td (P )),                        (C.1)
then there is a set E ⊆ G, depending on f, g, J, with |E| ≤ e−p N , such that

                En f − (1 + ϵ)g (n)J(n + h)T ′ (n) ≤ e−p             / E, T ′ ∈ Td−1 (p)).
                                  
                                                                  (h ∈                                 (C.2)

The exceptional set is chosen before T ′ .

    The proof is given in Proposition C.6 for d = 1 and Proposition C.12 for higher degrees. The
latter uses the structural results of Section A and Lemma B.1.



                                                    100
C.1    The degree-one argument
For a finite set Ω of characters of G and 0 < w ≤ 1, put

                        B(Ω, w) = {x ∈ G : |χ(x) − 1| ≤ w for every χ ∈ Ω}.

We use these symmetric common-width Bohr sets, retaining Ω and w(B) = w as part of the data;
their rank is D = |Ω|. The whole group has the presentation with one trivial character and width
one. A regular Bohr set B of rank D ≥ 1 satisfies

                              |B1+t | = (1 + O(D|t|))|B|       (|t| ≤ c/D).

Here dilating a Bohr set means dilating its defining widths. The usual covering argument gives
|B|/N ≥ (w(B)/10)D and |Bρ | ≥ (ρ/C)CD |B| for 0 < ρ < 1; a regular dilate exists between any
two scales whose ratio is two. Also, if v ∈ Bρ , regularity gives

                                       ∥µB (· − v) − µB ∥1 ≪ Dρ,                                     (C.3)

Here µB (x) = 1B (x)/|B| is the probability mass function on B, and the norm in (C.3) is the
unnormalized sum over G. Regular dilation and the following almost-periodicity input preserve the
common-width convention. Widths will be tracked explicitly; adding characters does not permit a
width-independent estimate for the relative size of the resulting Bohr set.
    The convolution almost-periodicity method originates with Croot and Sisask [12]. We use the
following radius-sensitive Bohr-set form of Schoen and Sisask.
Lemma C.2 (Almost-periodicity with a width bound). Fix 0 < η < 1/2. If S, S ′ are regular of rank
D ≥ 1, A1 ⊆ S, A2 ⊆ s0 + S ′ , both relative densities are at least e−q with q ≥ 1, and |K| ≤ 2|S|,
then, for independent uniform ai ∈ Ai , the probability that a1 − a2 belongs to K changes by at most
η after adding an independent uniform element of a regular Bohr set D∗ ⊆ S ′ with

           rank D∗ ≤ D + Cη (1 + q)4 ,       w(D∗ ) ≥ w(S ′ ) exp −Cη (1 + q + log(2 + D)) .
                                                                                             
                                                                                                     (C.4)

Proof. We apply Schoen–Sisask [43, Theorem 5.4], which uses unnormalized counting convolution.
In its notation, a regular Bohr set of rank d and radius ρ, a subset of relative density greater than
σ, a sumset bound |A + S0 | ≤ K1 |A|,
                                    √ and β = |M |/|L| ≤ 1 give a regular sub-Bohr set of rank at
most d + d′ and radius at least cρη β/(d2 d′ ), where

                       d′ ≤ Cη −2 log2 (2/(ηβ)) log(2/β) log(2K1 ) + C log(1/σ).

The error in its three-fold convolution is at most η|A||M |.
    Write αi for the two relative densities, and take a regular B0 = Sτ′ with τ ≍ 1/D sufficiently
small. Use B0 as the ambient Bohr set, and set A = −A2 , S0 = B0 . The increment set S0 has
density one in B0 , so σ = 1/2 is valid; regularity gives |A + S0 | ≤ 2|S ′ | ≤ (2/α2 )|A|. If |K| ≥ |A1 |,
take M = A1 , L = −K; then α1 /2 ≤ β ≤ 1. If η|A1 | ≤ |K| < |A1 |, interchange M and L; then
η ≤ β ≤ 1, and the error is still at most η|A1 ||A2 |. In either case d′ ≤ Cη (1 + q)4 , and the radius
lower bound implies (C.4). The counting convolution at zero, divided by |A1 ||A2 |, is the probability
in the statement; symmetry of D∗ gives the asserted smoothing estimate. Finally, if |K| < η|A1 |,
every translate of the difference law assigns K probability at most |K|/|A1 | < η. Taking D∗ = B0
proves this case as well.

    Only fixed η > 0 is used here. All convolution normalizations in the following local calculation
are stated explicitly.

                                                   101
Lemma C.3 (A bilinear reduction). Let a = f − (1 + ϵ)g. In degree one it suffices to prove

                        Ex,y H(x)W (y)a(x + y) ≤ e−4p            (0 ≤ H, W ≤ 1).                  (C.5)

Proof. If the conclusion fails on a set E of more than e−p N shifts, then En a(n)J(n + h) > e−p for
every h ∈ E: a constant test cannot create a positive violation where this inequality fails. Take
H = e−p J and W (y) = 1E (−y). The left side of (C.5) equals e−p Eh 1E (h)En a(n)J(n + h) > e−3p , a
contradiction.

The refinement to be proved. To prove the bilinear estimate, we decompose its two independent
averages into smaller Bohr cells. The difficulty is that a restriction can concentrate either weight.
We measure both weights by a concave potential, so that concentration and genuine smoothing each
pay for the further restrictions they require.
   Write a = f − (1 + ϵ)g. A node consists of a regular Bohr set B, two translates xB + B, yB + B,
and functions H, W ∈ [0, 1] supported on these translates. Define

 I = Ex∈xB +B, y∈yB +B H(x)W (y)a(x + y), u = Ex∈xB +B H(x), v = Ey∈yB +B W (y), Φ = (uv)1/4 .

Every node average uses the full size of its cell, even after a weight has been restricted and extended
by zero. In particular, I ≤ ep uv ≤ ep Φ. The original bilinear average is the node with B = G.
    We shall construct a refinement with successor nodes indexed by ν and nonnegative coefficients
cν such that                    X                       X
                           I≤      cν Iν + εerr Φ,         cν Φν ≤ (1 − γ)Φ.                       (C.6)
                                 ν                         ν

Here γ > 0 depends only on ϵ, and εerr can be any sufficiently small fixed multiple of e−10p .
Terms which need no further refinement are included in the error in the first inequality. The same
construction controls the geometry: if D and w are the parent’s stored rank and width, every
successor has
                                                       w
                         Dν ≤ D + (2 + p)Aϵ ,      log     ≤ (2 + p + D)Bϵ .                   (C.7)
                                                       wν
We need this refinement only for descendants of the root during R = ⌈cdepth p⌉ rounds. The geometric
bounds above forecast polynomial upper bounds for the ranks and log(1/w) of these nodes and
their intermediate dilates. Choose P to cover those bounds; the refinement is then uniform over
every center and pair of weights encountered in the run.
    There are two reasons to stop part of a node. If either cell mean is tiny, its entire positive
contribution is small relative to Φ. If a localized convolution is close to 1 in a sufficiently high
moment, positive comparison with a smooth Bohr test makes that localized contribution small.
The other parts become successors. A highly concentrated restriction is paid for by the potential
lost when that restriction is removed. In the absence of such concentration, a large convolution
moment yields almost-periods and forces a fixed decrease of the average potential. The following
two elementary estimates quantify these last two mechanisms.

    The local moment argument and the weighted dependent-random-choice step below adapt the

Lemma C.5 (Local convolution alternatives). Let L, S be symmetric regular Bohr sets, with S a

Proposition C.6 (Degree-one shift comparison). Theorem C.1 holds for d = 1.

Proof. We construct the refinement in (C.6), with the geometry in (C.7) and the full-cell averaging
convention fixed above. Put θ = 1/4.
    Fix K so large that θK 1−θ > 2, and choose c0 > 0 with (c0 K)θ ≤ 1/2. Choose κ = e−ccut p
                        ′
and ζ = exp(−(2 + p)Cϵ ) sufficiently small. Choose ccut = ccut (ϵ) first to control the tiny-node
error below. The moment constant and the number of rounds will be chosen afterwards; all are
independent of the node rank. If min(u, v) < κ, stop the node, since its positive contribution is at
most
                                        ep uv ≤ ep κ1−θ Φ.                                   (C.14)
   Here and throughout this proof, the required scale choices are regular dilates whose logarithmic
                                                                                                  ′′
width losses are polynomial in p + D, where D = rank B. Successor ranks are at most D + (2 + p)Cϵ .
To make the set of admissible successor shapes precise, retain w = w(B) and allow all regular
presentations C satisfying

         C ⊆ Bcζ/(2+D) ,      rank C ≤ D + (2 + p)Aϵ ,          w(C) ≥ w exp(−(2 + p + D)Bϵ ).

Choose Aϵ from (C.4), which will be applied with q = Oϵ (p2 ). Choose Bϵ after the exponent defining
ζ, large enough to cover that almost-periodicity width loss and the bounded number of regular
dilations within one refinement. These choices precede peeling. The exponent defining P is chosen
last.
    Removing concentration. While a translate of an admissible C has mean u1 > Ku of H, remove
the whole slice from H. Set b = |C|/|B|. The removed contribution is represented, up to O(ep ζb),
by nodes of shape C with coefficient b: keep the first center fixed, average the second center in its
parent cell, and add an independent uniform element of C on that side. This error bound is (C.3),
applied to the second parent cell. The second means average to at most v. Consequently their
total potential is at most b(u1 v)θ . Inequality (C.8) pays a factor greater than two for this potential.
Perform the same operation if W has such a slice.
    Stop immediately if either current mean becomes smaller than κ. At every other removal, the
removed mass is at least Kκbmin > 0, where the volume bound and admissibility give
                                                                       !D+(2+p)Aϵ
                                             w exp(−(2 + p + D)Bϵ )
                     b ≥ |C|/N ≥ bmin :=                                            .
                                                      10

Thus peeling terminates in finitely many steps. Moreover its errors are summable without multiplying
by the number of steps: since u1 , v ≥ κ, each b is at most κ−2θ b(u1 v)θ , and (C.8) telescopes. It
remains to treat a non-tiny remainder for which every admissible translate has H-mean at most Ku
and W -mean at most Kv.
    Matching two local scales. Choose tiny regular dilates S ≪ L ≪ B. Choose x0 , y0 independently
and uniformly in the two parent cells, take r ∈ L and s = s1 + s2 with s1 , s2 ∈ S independently
uniform, and put
                                    x = x0 + r,    y = y0 − r + s.
The resulting average differs from the parent average by O(ep ζ), by (C.3) twice. Restrict both
weights to x0 + L and y0 + L; the further error is O(ep ζ) because s is on a much smaller scale than
L. Let u0 , v0 be the resulting means on L. The absence of concentration gives u0 ≤ Ku, v0 ≤ Kv;
independence of x0 , y0 and concavity give

                                        Ex0 ,y0 (u0 v0 )θ ≤ (uv)θ .                               (C.15)

                                                   104
    One can return to independent cells of any sufficiently small common shape C by adding
independent t1 , t2 ∈ C to x, y. In (r, s) coordinates this translates the law by (t1 , t1 + t2 ), so its
total variation changes by O(ζ). If U, V are the new cell means, then, exactly,

                          Er,s U ≤ u0 ,   Er,s V ≤ v0 ,     Er,s (U V )θ ≤ (u0 v0 )θ .            (C.16)

For example, for each fixed s, t2 , summing the zero-extended second weight over −L + s + t2 is at
most its sum over the whole group, namely |L|v0 . This proves the second inequality; the first is
identical and the last follows by Cauchy–Schwarz and concavity.
    If u0 < c0 u or v0 < c0 v, return to independent cells in this way. The total potential of these
centers is at most (c0 K)θ Φ ≤ Φ/2. For the other centers u0 , v0 ≥ c0 κ. Fix one and normalize its
truncated weights to functions F, G on L of mean one. Their caps are at most M = (c0 κ)−1 . Choose
m = ⌈cmom p⌉ with cmom = cmom (ϵ, ccut ) large enough, and apply Lemma C.5 with sufficiently small
fixed δ = δ(ϵ).
    The flat alternative terminates a matched term. Its integral, divided by u0 v0 , is Es∼ν+ a(x0 + y0 +
s)(F ∗ G)(s). Outside a set of ν+ -measure at most 2−2m , |F ∗ G − 1| ≤ 2δ. Since 0 ≤ F ∗ G ≤ M ,
the exceptional part costs at most O(ep (M + 1)2−2m ). On the complementary set use

                              a(F ∗ G) ≤ (1 + 2δ)f − (1 + ϵ)(1 − 2δ)g.

Choose δ so that (1 + ϵ)(1 − 2δ) ≥ 1 + 2δ.
    Hypothesis (C.1) gives Es∼ν+ (f − g)(x0 + y0 + s) ≤ η for any prescribed η = exp(−(2 + p)Oϵ (1) ),
after taking P sufficiently large. Here is the needed approximation justification. Approximate a
translated regular Bohr indicator from above and below by [0, 1]-valued Lipschitz functions on
its defining torus, with transition width ρw(S). Their difference has normalized group mean at
most O(Dρ)|S|/N , and their Lipschitz constants are at most O(D/(ρw(S))). These are degree-one
niltests: the torus has lattice ZD and the orbit is given by the defining characters. With α = |S|/N ,
the hypothesis and the signed comparison yield

                       Es∈S (f − g)(z + s) ≤ α−1 e−P + O(ep Dρ)               (z ∈ G).

The width and rank bookkeeping below bounds both log(1/w(S)) and log(1/α) polynomially in p.
Thus ρ can be chosen with polynomial logarithmic loss to make the last display at most η, with all
test complexities at most P . Averaging over the second copy of S proves the assertion for ν+ . Thus
this node has positive contribution at most an arbitrarily small multiple of e−10p (u0 v0 )θ .
    The unbalanced alternative contracts the potential. Let Q be the function supplied by (C.11),
and write q = 2m′ = Oϵ (p). Choose a tiny regular shrink S ′ of S. Regularity, with error small after
multiplication by M 2q , supplies an s0 ∈ S such that

                               Ea∈S, b∈s0 +S ′ (Q ◦ Q)(a − b)q ≥ (1 + c)q

for a fixed c > 0. Set K0 = {Q ◦ Q ≥ 1 + c/2} ∩ (S − s0 − S ′ ). Regularity gives |K0 | ≤ 2|S|.
    For dependent random choice with these real weights, let L+ be a slight dilate of L containing L−a
for every a ∈ S ∪ (s0 + S ′ ). The scale separation and regularity ensure |L+ | ≤ 2|L|. Independently
sample t1 , . . . , tq uniformly from L+ . Given these samples, include each a ∈ S in A1 , and each
b ∈ s0 + S ′ in A2 , independently, with probabilities
                                   q                        q
                                   Y Q(tj + a)              Y Q(tj + b)
                                                  ,                       .
                                   j=1
                                           M                j=1
                                                                   M

The expected pair weight is |L|/(|L+ |M 2 ) (Q ◦ Q)(a − b)q . The contribution of pairs outside K0 ,
relative to the lower bound for all pairs, is at most ((1 + c/2)/(1 + c))q . By increasing m by a
fixed factor, this is smaller than any chosen fixed constant. To justify the simultaneous density and
bad-pair bounds, write Z = |A1 ||A2 |/(|S||S ′ |) and let Zbad be the same normalized count restricted
to differences outside K0 . The preceding estimates give EZ ≥ exp(−Oϵ (p2 )) and EZbad ≤ δ∗ EZ,
where δ∗ can be made any sufficiently small fixed constant by increasing the fixed multiple defining

                                          Z − C∗ Zbad ≥ 21 EZ.

It follows that Z ≥ exp(−Oϵ (p2 )) and Zbad /Z ≤ C∗−1 . Each relative density is at least Z, since both
are at most one. Thus both densities have the required lower bound and an arbitrarily high fixed
fraction of the differences lie in K0 .
    Apply (C.4) to these sets. Choose a tiny regular shrink C of D∗ so that adding independent
opposite shifts from C changes the uniform law on D∗ by O(ζ). If d is uniform on D∗ and c1 , c2 are

                         Ea∈A1 ,b∈A2 ,d,c1 ,c2 1K0 (a − b + d + c1 − c2 ) ≥ 1 − η0 ,

|A|−1 t∈A v(x + t); these averaging operators do not use the |L|−1 normalization of function-
      P

function convolution. Since Q ◦ Q ≥ 1 + c/2 on K0 and is nonnegative everywhere, the last display

                        max PC P−C (Q ◦ Q)(x) ≥ (1 + c/2)(1 − η0 ) ≥ 1 + c′
                          x

for a fixed c′ > 0. This function is the autocorrelation of PC Q, with sums normalized by |L|.
Cauchy–Schwarz puts its maximum at zero. Its mass outside L contributes O(M 2 ζ) to the second

   Return to independent cells of shape C, with centers x0 + r and y0 − r + s, where r is uniform
on L and s ∼ ν+ independently. Their means satisfy

                              U/u0 = PC F (r),        V /v0 = PC G(−r + s).

This shape is admissible: its rank increase is polynomial in p, and its logarithmic width loss relative

a fixed factor q0 < 1.
    Summing the refinements. Let A be the matched potential from small-ratio centers and B1
that from the other centers. We have A ≤ Φ/2 and A + B1 ≤ Φ, so the surviving potential is at
most A + q0 B1 ≤ (1 + q0 )Φ/2. For completeness, let Speel be the total weighted potential already
extracted by peeling, and Rrem the potential of its remainder. Telescoping gives 2Speel + Rrem ≤ Φ.
The matching argument contracts the surviving remainder potential by λ = (1 + q0 )/2 < 1; a tiny
remainder has no successors. Hence the total successor potential is at most

                                    Speel + λRrem ≤ max(1/2, λ)Φ.

This gives a fixed γ = γ(ϵ) > 0 such that all successors of a node have total weighted potential at
most (1 − γ)Φ. Their integrals bound its integral from above up to cϵ e−10p Φ, where cϵ can be made

                                                    106
arbitrarily small. Indeed all errors before a tiny-mean cutoff are bounded by fixed powers of ep κ−1
times ζ; the extracted errors have already been summed by (C.8).
     This proves (C.6) with εerr = cϵ e−10p ; admissibility of the successor shapes gives (C.7).
     Apply the refinement for R = ⌈cdepth p⌉ rounds. After r rounds, the total weighted potential of
the remaining nodes is at most (1 − γ)r Φroot . Hence the sum of all refinement errors is at most
εerr Φroot /γ, and the remaining positive integral is at most

                                                ep (1 − γ)R Φroot .

Since Φroot ≤ 1, choosing cdepth sufficiently large and then the permitted error constant sufficiently
small makes their sum less than e−4p .
    It remains to justify one comparison budget for the whole refinement. Along every branch, (C.7)
gives
              Dr+1 ≤ Dr + (2 + p)Aϵ ,       log(1/wr+1 ) ≤ log(1/wr ) + (2 + p + Dr )Bϵ .
Starting with D0 = 1, w0 = 1, both Dr and log(1/wr ) are polynomial in p for r ≤ R. The same is
true of the intermediate dilates used inside a round. The Bohr volume bound |B|/N ≥ (w(B)/10)D
consequently gives a polynomial bound for log(N/|B|). One choice P = (2 + p)C therefore covers
the smooth-test approximations, their required errors, and the size cutoff at every node. This proves
the bilinear estimate and, by Lemma C.3, degree-one shift comparison.

C.2    The finite-list reduction
For the induction from degree r to degree r + 1, a violating positive test of degree r may be chosen
separately at every shift. We cannot take a union bound over all these tests. Instead we will recover
them, on a sufficiently large set of shifts, from two fixed finite lists of positive tests: one observed
at n, the other at n + h. The remaining factor may depend on h, but has degree at most r − 1.
Degree-r shift comparison is uniform over this remaining factor, so only the two fixed lists enter
the union bound. The next lemma gives the precise reduction. The rest of the proof constructs its
factorization.

Lemma C.7 (Why fixed lists suffice). Assume shift comparison at degree r ≥ 1. Let 0 < ϵ < 1,
0 ≤ f, g, J ≤ ep , and suppose that N is odd and N ≥ exp(P ) for the sufficiently large polynomial
budget P in the conclusion. Suppose, for a set H ⊆ G of density at least σ, that positive tests Th
satisfy                                       
                               En f − (1 + ϵ)g (n)J(n + h)Th (n) > δ.
Suppose there are fixed families Ai , Bj ∈ Td (Q), d = r + 1, of sizes at most F , and, for each h ∈ H,
an approximation
                         M
                         X
              Th (n) =         Ai(ℓ,h) (n)Bj(ℓ,h) (n + h)Cℓ,h (n) + eh (n),   Cℓ,h ∈ Tr−1 (Q),
                         ℓ=1

with E|eh | ≤ δe−2p /6. Here all factors take values in [0, 1]. If

                     Q + log(F + M + 2) + log(1/σ) + log(1/δ) ≤ (2 + p)Od,ϵ (1) ,

then (C.1), with P a sufficiently large polynomial in p, rules out these violations.




                                                       107
Proof. Choose q ≥ max(p, Q) polynomially large so that F 2 e−q < σ and M e−q < δ/2. For each
fixed i, j, apply degree-r shift comparison to f Ai , gAi , JBj , with multiplicative slack ϵ/2 and budget
q. Its hypothesis follows from (C.1): multiplying a degree-r test by Ai gives a degree-d positive test,
of complexity bounded polynomially in the required budgets. There are at most F 2 fixed pairs, so
some h ∈ H lies outside every exceptional set. Uniformity over all lower tests then bounds each
summand by e−q . Replacing f − (1 + ϵ/2)g by f − (1 + ϵ)g only decreases the integral. Finally
|(f − (1 + ϵ)g)J| ≤ 3e2p , so the approximation error costs at most δ/2. This contradicts the displayed
violation.

C.3     Modeling and a common anchor
To construct the fixed lists, we first approximate each input by a finite linear combination of niltests
in a seminorm adapted to the shifted comparisons. The separation argument is a form of the
Hahn–Banach method for deriving decompositions from inverse theorems [19, Section 3.2]; the
explicit finite list is then obtained by sampling. On a finite probability space, the extended dual of
a seminorm X is
                                     X ∗ (ψ) = sup |⟨v, ψ⟩| ∈ [0, ∞].
                                                    X(v)≤1

It is finite precisely when ψ annihilates ker X. A family is balanced if it is closed under multiplication
by complex scalars of modulus at most one.
Lemma C.8 (Modeling from a seminorm detector). Let Ω be a finite probability space, let X be
a seminorm on CΩ with X(v) ≤ K∥v∥1 , and let S be a nonempty compact, balanced collection of
functions of supremum at most one. Fix M, K ≥ 1, β > 0, and 0 < τ < 1. Suppose that every ψ
satisfying
                              ∥ψ∥∞ ≤ K/τ,      X(ψ) ≥ τ /M 2
has |⟨ψ, Q⟩| ≥ β for some Q ∈ S. Then every b with ∥b∥∞ ≤ M has a representation
                                 ℓ
                                 X                      X
                            b=         ci Qi + e,             |ci | ≤ 2/β,   X(e) ≤ 2τ,
                                 i=1                     i

with ℓ ≤ 1 + 4K 2 /(β 2 τ 2 ).
Proof. Write R = 2/β. Suppose that b does not belong to R conv(S) + {e : X(e) ≤ τ }. Separation
in the underlying finite-dimensional real vector space gives a nonzero ψ with

                                       Re⟨b, ψ⟩ > R sup |⟨Q, ψ⟩| + τ X ∗ (ψ).
                                                        Q∈S

The right side is positive; normalize it to one. Then X ∗ (ψ) ≤ 1/τ . Since X(v) ≤ K∥v∥1 , testing on
point masses yields ∥ψ∥∞ ≤ K/τ . Also ∥ψ∥2 > 1/M , by the strict lower bound on Re⟨b, ψ⟩. Duality
gives X(ψ)X ∗ (ψ) ≥ ∥ψ∥22 and consequently X(ψ) > τ /M 2 . The detection hypothesis contradicts
R supQ |⟨Q, ψ⟩| ≤ 1. The indicated convex sum therefore exists with X(e) ≤ τ . Its structural part
has ℓ1 coefficient norm at most R. Sample ℓ independent terms with probabilities proportional to
their coefficient magnitudes, absorbing their phases into S. The expected squared L2 error of the
empirical average is at most R2 /ℓ. Take ℓ ≥ R2 K 2 /τ 2 and use X ≤ K∥ · ∥2 .

    In the application to shift comparison,

                                   X(e) = Eh sup |En e(n)B(n + h)L(n)| ,                           (C.17)
                                                    L


                                                             108
where L ranges over complex, unit-bounded degree-r niltests of controlled complexity and all their
cyclic translates. Thus the family includes n 7→ L0 (n + t), with addition taken in G, even when its
representative path wraps. The supremum allows a different test and translation at each h. It is
a seminorm and X(e) ≤ ∥B∥∞ ∥e∥1 . Choosing an approximately maximizing test and aligning its
phase, then applying Cauchy–Schwarz at n + h, gives

                    X(e)2 ≤ ∥B∥2∞ Eh,k En e(n)e(n + h − k)Lh (n)Lk (n + h − k) .                   (C.18)

The product in (C.18) contains at most two translated representative niltests. Lemma A.9 therefore
applies uniformly, and the derivative identity gives a quantitative U r+2 detector. The quasipolynomial
inverse theorem [35] then supplies the structural tests for Lemma C.8. These structural tests are
ordinary degree-(r + 1) niltests; only the testing family has been enlarged. The translated family
permits the change of variable m = n+h when modeling the second input: Lh (n) becomes Lh (m−h).
The supremum handles these translations directly, without a union bound over them. All cutoff
errors in the converse estimate are chosen below the detection threshold.
Lemma C.9 (A common anchor). Let H be a finite nonempty set. For 1 ≤ j ≤ m and h ∈ H,
let vh,j be complex functions of supremum at most one on finite probability spaces Ωj . Suppose
|Evh,j | ≥ δ > 0 for all h, j. There exist h0 ∈ H and H ′ ⊆ H with |H ′ | ≥ δ 2m |H|/2 such that

                            |Evh,j vh0 ,j | ≥ δ 2m /2     (h ∈ H ′ , 1 ≤ j ≤ m).

Proof. Multiply each vh,j by a unit scalar so its mean is real and nonnegative, and tensor the
functions in their independent variables to form Vh on j Ωj . Then EVh ≥ δ m and ∥Vh ∥2 ≤ 1.
                                                           Q

Consequently
                               Eh,k Re⟨Vh , Vk ⟩ = ∥Eh Vh ∥22 ≥ δ 2m .
Choose h0 with the same lower bound after averaging only h. At least a δ 2m /2 fraction have real
inner product at least δ 2m /2. For these h, the absolute inner product is a product of m numbers in
[0, 1], so each factor is at least δ 2m /2. Removing the aligning phases changes no absolute value.

C.4    Recovering tests from two fixed observations
The next lemma records precisely what positive discretization can do after the requisite geometry
has been established.
Lemma C.10 (Positive discretization). Fix finite families of nonnegative functions {Ai }i∈I and
{Bj }j∈J on G, each summing to one. For each shift h, suppose that, outside a set Eh ,

                                          Th (n) = Fh (zh (n), ξh (n)),

where 0 ≤ Fh ≤ 1 and Fh is L-Lipschitz in its second variable. Suppose that whenever Ai (n)Bj (n+h)
and Ai (n′ )Bj (n′ + h) are positive, with n, n′ ∈  / Eh , one has d(ξh (n), ξh (n′ )) ≤ ρ. Then there are
functions Cij,h (n) = Fh (zh (n), ξij,h ) such that

                                   X
                        Th (n) −         Ai (n)Bj (n + h)Cij,h (n) ≤ Lρ   (n ∈
                                                                             / Eh ).               (C.19)
                                   i,j

An empty cell pair may be assigned Cij,h = 0. If the first-variable sections of Fh are bounded
Lipschitz functions on a degree-(r − 1) nilmanifold and zh is a polynomial path on it, then every
Cij,h is a degree-(r − 1) niltest with the corresponding uniform complexity.

                                                        109
Proof. For every nonempty cell pair select a point nij,h ∈  / Eh where its weight is positive and put
ξij,h = ξh (nij,h ). For each contributing pair the replacement error is at most Lρ. Sum against the
nonnegative weights, whose sum is one. The final assertion follows by restricting Fh to the chosen
second coordinate.

Lemma C.11 (Uniform recovery after right normalization). Fix controlled rational nilpotent groups,
lattices, and a rational subgroup V of their product. Fix a controlled lattice ΛV on V . There are
fixed fine covers of the input lattices with the following property. Fix bounded left translations
and arbitrary right translations by elements of the original input lattices, and apply these same
translations to both of two tuples. Suppose that both normalized tuples belong to V . If the two
original tuples are within ρ on the fixed input covers, then their normalized images are within B(p)ρ
on V /ΛV , provided ρ ≤ B(p)−1 . The covers and constants do not depend on the right translations.
The left translations may instead differ by at most ρ in each coordinate; the same conclusion holds
after enlarging its constant. One may additionally require compatibility with right translation by
rational elements having a fixed common denominator q ≤ B(p).

Proof. First choose a fine ambient product lattice Λ0 such that V ∩ Λ0 ⊆ ΛV . The restriction of
the ambient quotient metric has a controlled local inverse on V /(V ∩ Λ0 ). To see this directly,
reduce both points using V ∩ Λ0 to bounded representatives. An ambient lattice element witnessing
closeness is then bounded. Its logarithm lies in a controlled rational grid; if its distance from the
rational subspace log V is below the rational separation bound, it lies in that subspace exactly. It
therefore belongs to V ∩ Λ0 . Controlled coordinate changes compare the ambient and intrinsic small
distances. This is also the metric argument in Lemma A.3.
    Choose each input cover Λ inside the corresponding factor of Λ0 , normal in the original lattice Γ.
Here is an explicit construction. If log Γ ⊆ l−1 ZD and m0 ZD ⊆ log Λ0 , choose a controlled integer
m divisible by lm0 , and set
                                          Λ = ⟨γ m : γ ∈ Γ⟩.
Every generator lies in Λ0 because log(γ m ) = m log γ ∈ m0 ZD . This subgroup is characteristic in Γ.
A Mal’cev normal form with D coordinates gives [Γ : Λ] ≤ mD by reducing each exponent modulo
m; powers of the Mal’cev generators give controlled grids for Λ. Thus all logarithmic index and
coordinate costs remain polynomial.
    For one coordinate, let a = ϵ−1 xγ −1 and a′ = ϵ−1 x′ γ −1 , with ϵ bounded and γ ∈ Γ. If
dG (xλ, x′ ) ≤ ρ for λ ∈ Λ, then

                    a(γλγ −1 ) = ϵ−1 xλγ −1 ,    dG a(γλγ −1 ), a′ ≤ Lip(Lϵ−1 )ρ.
                                                                  


Normality puts γλγ −1 in Λ ⊆ Λ0 . Thus normalized ambient distances are controlled uniformly in γ.
Apply the preceding local inverse on V . If the bounded left normalizers are ϵ and ϵ′ , right invariance
gives
                            dG (ϵ−1 x′ γ −1 , (ϵ′ )−1 x′ γ −1 ) = dG (ϵ−1 , (ϵ′ )−1 ).
Inversion is controlled on the bounded set of normalizers, so their variation adds at most B(p)ρ
before applying the local inverse. No orbit representative enters this error bound.
    For rational right translations, the adjoint formula has a common denominator bounded in terms
of q and the structural data; its numerators need not be bounded. Choose the earlier congruence
strides sufficiently divisible to force the required conjugates into Λ0 . This requires no bound on
those numerators. Again normal refinement inside Γ handles arbitrary lattice right translations at
the same time.



                                                  110
Proposition C.12 (Higher-degree propagation). Lemmas A.3, A.6, A.8, A.9, and B.1, together
with the quasipolynomial inverse theorem, imply the propagation principle in Theorem C.1 for every
fixed degree.
Proof. Induct on d, using Proposition C.6 for the base case. Set r = d − 1 ≥ 1 and a = f − (1 + ϵ)g.
Suppose that a set of more than e−p N shifts has a violating positive degree-r test Th . All losses
in this proof have logarithm polynomial in p; the degree and the multiplicative slack are fixed. A
precision called “sufficiently small” below is at least exp(−P(p)). No list is obtained by discretizing
the functions f, g, J or the coefficients of a polynomial orbit.
     Structural models at the two sites. For the seminorm (C.17), (C.18) implies the needed detector
as follows. A lower bound X(e) ≥ τ , after normalizing its cap, gives a proportion B(p)−1 of pairs
(h, k) with derivative correlation at least B(p)−1 . The product of translated tests in that correlation
is covered by Lemma A.9, including representative wraps. Thus a proportion B(p)−1 of differences
h − k has derivative U r+1 norm at least B(p)−1 : the difference is uniform, and each fixed difference
has exactly N pairs. The Gowers derivative identity gives ∥e∥U r+2 ≥ B(p)−1 . The quasipolynomial
inverse theorem therefore gives a degree-d correlator of polynomial logarithmic complexity and
correlation B(p)−1 .
    Apply Lemma C.8 with these detectors. To fit its compactness hypothesis, take the closure in the
finite-dimensional space of values on G of the balanced family of allowed niltests. The detector still
supplies an actual niltest. After obtaining the finite model, approximate its finitely many closure
points by actual niltests to any prescribed further L2 accuracy. The cap bound X ≤ ∥B∥∞ ∥ · ∥2
controls this additional error. Thus a bounded function at either site has, in the corresponding
seminorm, a model with list length and ℓ1 coefficient sum at most B(p).
     Positive Fourier projection and fixed partners. Write Th = Fh (Ph (n)Γh ). Truncate its top-torus
Fourier expansion at sufficiently high controlled precision. Declare a frequency significant if the
corresponding vertical term has correlation against a(n)J(n + h) larger in absolute value than a
sufficiently small controlled threshold. Average Fh over the connected annihilator of the rational
span of the significant frequencies. This is a positive averaging operator, so the resulting test still
lies in [0, 1]. It changes the tested integral by at most the Fourier truncation error plus the number
of discarded truncated frequencies times the significance threshold. Hence a positive violation of size
B(p)−1 remains. Replace Fh and Th by these averages and retain their notation. Each significant
vertical mode is unchanged by this replacement.
     Choose a basis ηh,1 , . . . , ηh,m from the significant frequencies, with corresponding vertical terms
Uh,j . After pigeonholing, m is fixed and at most P(p). If m = 0, the averaged test factors through
the degree-(r − 1) quotient, and degree-r shift comparison already rules out the violations after a
polynomial enlargement of its budget. Thus assume m ≥ 1. Model a with other input J. Next
model J for each fixed structural term of that first model. Choose the second precision after the
first list and its coefficient bound have been fixed. For the latter application, write u = n + h and
use the shift −h: the fixed structural factor is evaluated at u − h, and the varying test Uh,j (u − h)
belongs to the translated testing family in (C.17). Markov’s inequality applied to the modeling
seminorm errors removes only a negligible proportion of shifts, uniformly for every selected Uh,j .
Expanding the two models shows that each remaining significant term has partners Qj , Q′j with

                                 En Uh,j (n)Qj (n)Q′j (n + h) ≥ B(p)−1 .                           (C.20)

The factors can be scaled to have cap one. Take vertical modes in the top-torus expansions of
both partners, and pigeonhole these choices for all j simultaneously. The number of choices is
B(p)P(p) = B(p). We retain a set of shifts of density B(p)−1 on which all partner maps and functions
are fixed.

                                                   111
   An anchor and a common rational factorization. Apply Lemma C.9 to the functions in (C.20),
tensoring in j. For one fixed h0 , a further set of density B(p)−1 satisfies, for every j,

                     En Uh,j (n)Uh0 ,j (n)|Qj (n)|2 Q′j (n + h)Q′j (n + h0 ) ≥ B(p)−1 .            (C.21)

For each j, divide the representative interval at the two wrap points in the shifted partners. Intervals
shorter than a sufficiently small B(p)−1 N have negligible total contribution. One remaining interval
has both relative length and normalized bias at least B(p)−1 . On it, extend the partners using their
corresponding ordinary integer shifts. Pigeonhole the finitely many anchor branches.
   Write Qj (n) = Vj (gj (n)Γj ) on Gj /Γj . Since Qj is vertical at degree d, |Qj |2 is a function of the
degree-r quotient path aj (n) = gj (n)(Gj )d in Gj = Gj /(Gj )d . Let sh,j (n) be the normalized square
path supplied by Lemma A.8 for the last two factors in (C.21), and let Sj be its degree-r quotient
group. Taking top Fourier modes of the functions on Gj and Sj selects a pair that retains bias
B(p)−1 on the selected interval. Their frequencies, denoted by αh,j and βh,j , give a unit-bounded
degree-r test on
                               Dh,j (n) = (Ph (n), Ph0 (n), aj (n), sh,j (n))
with combined top frequency
                                       Λh,j = (ηh,j , −ηh0 ,j , αh,j , βh,j ).
Apply Lemma B.1 to this biased test, obtaining a graded rational subalgebra Wh,j whose top layer
is annihilated by Λh,j .
    Writing Gh for the ambient group of Ph , use the common degree-r product map
                                                                                 m
                                                                                 Y
                (Ph , Zh ) = (Ph , Ph0 , (aj , sh,j )m
                                                     j=1 )   into   Gh × Gh0 ×       (Gj × Sj ).

Let ρj be its coordinate projection to the four factors of Dh,j . The inverse image of Wh,j under the
induced graded projection gr(ρj ) admits a splitting of the total symbol: lift the slow and rational
symbols by placing identities in the omitted coordinates. The selected intervals may differ with j,
but parameter translation leaves the symbol unchanged, and their lengths are at least B(p)−1 N .
Thus all these splittings have slow bounds at scale N , after enlarging the budget. Lemma A.6 gives
a simultaneous splitting through the intersection Uh of these inverse images. Its lift is an actual
factorization
                               (Ph , Zh ) = Eh bh Rh κh ,  bh (n) ∈ Hh ,                       (C.22)
with its refiltration (Hh )i ; Eh is fully slow, Rh is rational with bounded common denominator, and
κh is a constant lattice element.
   Write π for the projection to Zh . The total filtration has degree r, so (Hh )r = exp((Uh )r ), and
each Λh,j ◦ dρj annihilates (Uh )r . On
                                                                            
                                      Kh = ker (Hh )r −→ π((Hh )r ) ,                              (C.23)

all coordinates except the Gh coordinate are the identity. The frequency equation therefore says
that every ηh,j annihilates the logarithm of the first-coordinate projection of Kh . All frequencies of
the projected positive function belong to their rational span, so the function is invariant under Kh .
This kernel is the exponential of a rational subspace of the central top layer, hence is connected,
rational, and central in the total group.
    Fixing rational data before choosing covers. The partner groups and their square quotients are
fixed. The controlled rational group data for the first factor, the subgroups Hh , and their filtrations


                                                        112
and quotient maps range over at most B(p) possibilities: bases use polynomially many rational
entries, each of bounded height. The same finite bound applies to controlled lattice choices; one
may work with the integral congruence covers constructed in the preliminaries. Pigeonhole all
these rational choices, the common denominators of Rh , and any required congruence moduli. The
retained shift density is still B(p)−1 . We now write H for the fixed subgroup and regard all rational
diagrams as fixed. The polynomial orbits, bounded real constants, and Lipschitz functions may still
depend on h. We have not taken a common multiple over exponentially many alternative moduli.
    Freezing the slow and rational factors. Choose a fixed fine partition of the coordinate n/N into
cells of diameter ρ. On one such cell the slow factor Eh (n) varies by at most B(p)ρ; its values
and the values of its inverse are bounded by B(p). By rational polynomial periodicity there is a
fixed q ≤ B(p) such that, on each class n mod q, the right lattice class needed to evaluate the first
component of Rh (n) is constant. Use a bounded representative of that class. After freezing these
factors, the original test is, up to B(p)ρ, a controlled positive Lipschitz function of bh (n) on a fine
lattice cover of H. Its invariance under (C.23) persists, since that subgroup is central.
    Let GZ be the fixed full product group containing the other inputs Zh . Its dimension is
polynomially bounded and its filtration has degree at most r: its factors are the anchor degree-r
paths, the degree-r quotients of the unshifted partners, and the normalized square quotients. Use

                                H −→ H/Hr × GZ ,        b 7−→ (bHr , π(b)),
whose kernel is Kh = K, now fixed. Lemma A.3 represents the frozen function as a positive bounded
Lipschitz function of these two image coordinates on fine product covers, at cost B(p). Write Λ ≤ GZ
for the full ambient lattice in the second cover. Using GZ , rather than only π(H), is necessary
because the other factors in the projected factorization need not lie in π(H). Extend from the
image to the product metric space by Lipschitz extension and clip to [0, 1]. The first coordinate is a
polynomial path of degree r − 1. The remaining task is to recover the second coordinate from fixed
observations at n and n + h.
    For this recovery, also freeze the left lattice class of π(Rh (n)) for the full ambient target lattice Λ.
Its cocompactness supplies bounded rational representatives of these classes. Rational periodicity,
whose period depends only on the fixed structural data and common denominator, permits a single
refinement of q by a controlled amount, uniformly over all retained h and simultaneously with the
earlier right-class requirement. On a residue class write π(Rh (n)) = λn zh,c , with λn ∈ Λ and zh,c a
bounded rational representative. Projecting (C.22) then gives the exact identity
                                                                      −1
                             π(bh (n))Λ = π(Eh (n))−1 Zh (n)π(κh )−1 zh,c Λ.                         (C.24)

The order of these factors is essential. A right lattice class of Rh alone would not justify this identity.
    Recovering squares from fixed observations. The anchor paths and unshifted quotient paths in
Zh are fixed paths at n. A square path is obtained from two original partner paths by bounded left
normalization and a lattice right normalization, followed by the fixed subgroup inclusion and square
quotient. Lemma C.11 gives fixed input covers on which this operation is uniformly Lipschitz. In
particular, the arbitrary magnitude of its lattice normalizer does not enter the metric estimate.
                                                        −1
The same lemma makes the right factors π(κh )−1 zh,c       in (C.24) compatible with fixed finer covers.
For fixed h, residue class and wrap branches, both locations being compared use the same square
normalizers and the same right factors. The left slow inverses can differ by B(p)ρ; the varying-
left-normalizer estimate in Lemma C.11 controls the resulting error independently of the orbit
representatives.
    The ordinary extension of an anchored partner is a fixed path n 7→ gj′ (n + h0 − bj N ), with its


                                                    113
branch bj already fixed. An ordinary extension at the shifted site is one of the fixed paths

                       m 7−→ gj′ (m + tN ),        t ∈ {−1, 0, 1},   m = rep(n + h).

Which branch is used is determined by h and the coarse n/N cell except when that cell meets
the wrap point. Discard a neighborhood of width O(ρN ) around that point and the endpoint of
the interval-coordinate chart. This has relative size O(ρ + 1/N ), uniformly in h. There are only
polynomially many fixed paths, including every branch required for all partners.
    Positive partitions and the uniform oscillation estimate. At the first site measure those fixed
paths on their chosen covers, the coordinate n/N , and the residue n mod q. At the second site
measure all the fixed shifted-path alternatives. Take positive Lipschitz partitions of unity into cells
of diameter at most ρ on these two product spaces. The residue partition can be realized exactly on
the grid by a circle path n 7→ n/q mod 1, with Lipschitz cost O(q). The interval-coordinate partition
is realized similarly away from the discarded endpoint neighborhood. Since the total dimension and
log(1/ρ) are polynomial in p, both families have at most B(p) members and complexity at most
P(p). Denote them by Ai (n) and Bj (n + h); these are fixed degree-d niltests.
    For a fixed h, consider two good locations contributing to the same pair of cells. Their frozen
residue classes and wrap branches agree; their slow factors differ by at most B(p)ρ. Their fixed orbit
observations are within O(ρ) on the chosen covers. Lemma C.11, followed by (C.24), therefore gives

                                     d π(bh (n))Λ, π(bh (n′ ))Λ ≤ B(p)ρ.
                                                               
                                                                                                 (C.25)

This is a uniform estimate over all retained h, rather than a continuity assertion whose constant
may depend on h.
    Freeze the first-site slow value separately for each cell i. For every nonempty pair (i, j) choose a
good point and replace the second image coordinate in the extended reconstruction function by its
value there. The resulting function of bh (n)Hr is a positive degree-(r − 1) test Cij,h . The proof of
Lemma C.10, allowing the frozen function to be chosen separately for each first-site cell, gives
                       X
            Th (n) =         Ai (n)Bj (n + h)Cij,h (n) + eh (n),     E|eh | ≤ B(p)ρ + O(1/N ).
                       i,j

No sum of errors over all cell pairs is necessary, since their nonnegative weights sum to one. Choose
ρ small enough, and the length cutoff large enough, to make this error adequate for Lemma C.7.
All costs remain within P(p), B(p).
    The retained shift set and its positive violations both have controlled inverse-exponential lower
bounds. Lemma C.7 now contradicts them by degree-r shift comparison. Its finitely many fixed
pairs are chosen before that application; the lower tests Cij,h may depend arbitrarily on h because
the induction is uniform in those tests. Choosing budgets backwards through the fixed degree depth
gives a single polynomial P = (2 + p)C(d,ϵ) . This proves shift comparison in every fixed degree.


D     Positive counting and absolute increments
A function on an integer box is called progression-free when its support contains no tuple u, u +
v, . . . , u + (k − 1)v with v ∈ Zn \ {0} and all vertices in the box. The length k will always be fixed
in the statement using this term.
      Positive comparison first gives an absolute increment on a box of fixed dimension. We convert
its niltest to a patch and then state the relative lifting theorem, proved in Theorem I.15. That
theorem samples fixed-dimensional boxes and returns their increments with rank at most d + d0 .

                                                      114
Applied to the constant old patch, of rank d = 0, it gives the starting rule of Lemma 2.2: the fresh
rank d0 is independent of the ambient dimension. The triangular argument in the main proof must
additionally control the losses in the old widths; that assertion is not part of the relative lifting
theorem.

D.1    From positive comparison to counting
We use Theorem C.1 as an input. Its uniformity in the lower-degree test is part of the input. In
particular, the exceptional set may depend on the two positive functions but must not depend on
that test. Throughout this subsection the ambient group is H = Z/N Z. For positive counting at
length k we assume gcd(N, (k − 1)!) = 1, so every nonzero slope difference of absolute value at most
k − 1 is invertible in H. The grid lemma needs only that N be odd, as required by shift comparison.

Proposition D.3 (Absolute increment on a fixed-dimensional box). Assume Theorem C.1. Fix

D.2    Patches and the lifting statement
Definition D.4 (Patch). A degree-s patch on integer parameters u is a function
                              X
                     B(u) =          Φ(Su (b)),        Su (b)i = bi − Ai (u, b1 , . . . , bi−1 ).   (D.9)
                              b∈Zd

The slots bi have nondecreasing integer weights 1 ≤ wi ≤ s; parameters have weight 1; and Ai has
weighted degree at most wi . The function Φ : Rd → [0, 1] is Lipschitz and supported in [−1/4, 1/4]d .
The rank is d. A logarithmic complexity bound bounds the number of parameters, the rank, and
log(2 + Lip(Φ)), but does not bound the coefficients of the Ai . Rank zero includes B = 1.

    At most one summand in (D.9) is nonzero: first the condition on slot 1 determines its possible
integer uniquely, and then one continues in increasing slot order. In particular 0 ≤ B ≤ 1. Figure 1
shows a two-slot example.
    For B ≥ 0, a positive score E(f − a)B says that the mean of f under the probability weight
proportional to B exceeds a. Its explicit lower bound also records how much mass is available for

                                                        119
later restrictions and approximation errors. Rank and complexity have different roles. The relative
lifting theorem below bounds the returned rank by d + d0 . Its rank-zero application therefore
introduces at most d0 slots even when the resource budget is enlarged to include the ambient
dimension. The main iteration uses this fresh-rank bound together with its additive triangular
dimension recurrence. Other logarithmic costs may grow polynomially, with their compositions
accounted for explicitly.
     For the qualitative correspondence between generalized-polynomial coordinates and polynomial
nilmanifold orbits, see Bergelson and Leibman [3, Theorem A∗ ]. The conversion below gives the
quantitative bounds needed for our Lipschitz patches.

Lemma D.5 (Conversion of patches). For fixed s, a patch of rank d and logarithmic complexity p
is a niltest of degree at most s, nilmanifold dimension at most (1 + d)Cs , and logarithmic complexity
at most (2 + p)Cs . Conversely, the test in Proposition D.3 can be replaced by a single patch on the
original box, of rank and logarithmic complexity (2 + p)Cs,n , with score at least exp(−(2 + p)Cs,n ).

Proof. For the rational Mal’cev-coordinate calculus used here, see Green and Tao [23, Appendix A].
We give the triangular shear realization and its lattice bounds explicitly.
    From patches to niltests. Integer polynomial changes of the summation variables normalize the
coefficients of the Ai modulo integers, in increasing slot order. Indeed, replace bi by bi + Ri (u, b<i )
for an integer-coefficient polynomial Ri and substitute this change in the later slots. This is a
bijection of Zd for each integer u, preserves the residual vector, and preserves weighted degree. Thus
every coefficient can be put in [−1/2, 1/2] without changing Φ. A later polynomial may acquire
large coefficients during an earlier substitution; these are reduced when its own slot is reached, so
the final bound is independent of every original coefficient.
    Let S 0 be the part of Su homogeneous in the b variables of weight wi in slot i. It is independent
of u. Write Su = S 0 ◦ Yu . Then Yu belongs to the group Y of triangular polynomial transformations
whose addition in slot i has weight strictly less than wi . Filter this group by weight deficit. It is a
simply connected nilpotent group of step at most s and of dimension at most
                                                  !
                                          d+s
                                        d             ≤ (1 + d)Cs .
                                           s

For completeness, its Lie algebra has the monomial shears bβ ∂/∂bi with wt(β) < wi as a basis.
Such a shear has deficit wi − wt(β); a bracket has the sum of the deficits. The exponential of each
shear is its elementary triangular substitution. Ordering these shears by increasing deficit gives
triangular polynomial multiplication and inversion, with rational coefficients of bounded logarithmic
complexity. The integer-coefficient transformations form a cocompact lattice Γ; successive reduction
of their triangular coefficients gives bounded fundamental representatives. Equivalently, the ordered
elementary shears give integral Mal’cev coordinates.
    We verify both the logarithmic lattice grids and the precise polynomial condition in Definition A.1.
On the finite-dimensional space of polynomials in b of weight at most s, let Y ∗ denote substitution
by Y and put U = Y ∗ − I. Since U lowers weight, U s+1 = 0 and
                                                  s
                                                    (−1)h+1
                                      log Y ∗ =
                                                  X
                                                                U h.
                                                  h=1
                                                           h

Applied to bi , this gives the coefficients of the logarithmic shear field. For an integer-coefficient
transformation their denominators divide s!. Conversely, if a shear field X has all coefficients in s!Z,



                                                   120
                                Ps         h b /h! has integer coefficients. Writing D = dim Y, we obtain
